Iwasawa theory for branched ℤ p \mathbb{Z}_{p} -towers of finite graphs
Rusiru Gambheera, Daniel Vallières

Abstract
. We initiate the study of Iwasawa theory for branched ℤ p \mathbb{Z}_{p} -towers of finite connected graphs. These towers are more general than what have been studied so far, since the morphisms of graphs involved are branched covers, a particular kind of harmonic morphisms of graphs. We prove an analogue of Iwasawa’s asymptotic class number formula for the p p -part of the number of spanning trees in this setting. Moreover, we find an explicit generator for the characteristic ideal of the torsion Iwasawa module governing the growth of the p p -part of the number of spanning trees in such towers.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary: 05C25; Secondary: 11R23

1.
Introduction

Let
p
be a rational prime and let

K=K_{0}\subseteq K_{1}\subseteq K_{2}\subseteq\ldots\subseteq K_{n}\subseteq\ldots

be a tower of number fields for which
K_{n}/K
is Galois with Galois group isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}\mathbb{Z}
. Iwasawa’s famous asymptotic class number formula (
[
Iwa59
]
and
[
Iwa73
]
) shows the existence of
\mu,\lambda,n_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and
\nu\in\mathbb{Z}
such that when
n\geq n_{0}
, one has

{\rm ord}_{p}(h(K_{n}))=\mu p^{n}+\lambda n+\nu,

where
h(K_{n})
denotes the class number of
K_{n}
, and
{\rm ord}_{p}
denotes the usual
p
-adic valuation on the field of rational numbers.

Motivated by the analogy between number theory and graph theory, one can study the variation of the
p
-part of the number of spanning trees as one goes up a
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-tower of graphs

(1.1)

X=X_{0}\leftarrow X_{1}\leftarrow X_{2}\leftarrow\ldots\leftarrow X_{n}\leftarrow\ldots

Such a
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-tower consists of a sequence of covering maps
X_{n+1}\rightarrow X_{n}
between finite connected graphs for which the composition
X_{n}\rightarrow X_{n-1}\rightarrow\ldots\rightarrow X_{0}=X
is Galois with Galois group isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}\mathbb{Z}
. It turns out that in perfect analogy with Iwasawa’s asymptotic class number formula, there exist
\mu,\lambda,n_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and
\nu\in\mathbb{Z}
such that

(1.2)

{\rm ord}_{p}(\kappa(X_{n}))=\mu p^{n}+\lambda n+\nu,

provided
n\geq n_{0}
, where now
\kappa(X_{n})
is the number of spanning trees of
X_{n}
.

A proof of this result is given in increasing generality in
[
Val21
,
MV23
,
MV24
]
. The approach therein is based on Ihara zeta and
L
-functions and their special value at
u=1
. The Ihara zeta function
Z_{X}(u)
of a finite graph is the reciprocal of a polynomial. It is of the form
Z_{X}(u)^{-1}=(1-u^{2})^{-\chi(X)}h_{X}(u)
, where
\chi(X)
denotes the Euler characteristic of
X
, and where
h_{X}(u)
is some polynomial with integer coefficients given explicitly as the determinant of an operator via Ihara’s determinant formula. A result of Hashimoto (
[
Has90
]
) gives

h_{X}^{{}^{\prime}}(1)=-2\chi(X)\kappa(X),

when
X
is a finite connected graph. Therefore, the special value at
u=1
of these functions encodes much information about the invariant
\kappa(X)
. These facts can be used to construct a power series
f(T)\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}\llbracket T\rrbracket
, and combined with the Artin formalism satisfied by the Ihara
L
-functions, one can get the Iwasawa invariants
\mu
and
\lambda
from the power series
f(T)
thereby obtaining (
1.2
).

On the other hand, another proof of (
1.2
) has been obtained by Gonet in
[
Gon22
]
. Her approach is algebraic and makes use of the structure theorem for Iwasawa modules. To every finite connected graph is associated a finite abelian group
{\rm Pic}^{0}(X)
which is called the Picard group of degree zero of
X
(also known as the Jacobian, the sandpile, or still the critical group of
X
). It follows from a classical theorem of Kirchhoff in graph theory that its cardinality is precisely the invariant
\kappa(X)
. Any cover of finite connected graphs
f\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Y\rightarrow X
induces a surjective group morphism
f_{*}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}{\rm Pic}^{0}(Y)\rightarrow{\rm Pic}^{0}(X)
. Thus from a
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-tower of graphs such as in (
1.1
) above, one obtains a compatible system of maps
{\rm Pic}^{0}(X_{n+1})[p^{\infty}]\rightarrow{\rm Pic}^{0}(X_{n})[p^{\infty}]
, where we write
A[p^{\infty}]
for the Sylow
p
-subgroup of a finite abelian group
A
. Since each
{\rm Pic}^{0}(X_{n})[p^{\infty}]
is a
\mathbb{Z}_{p}[\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}\mathbb{Z}]
-module, the inverse limit

(1.3)

{\rm Pic}^{0}_{\Lambda}=\varprojlim_{n\geq 0}{\rm Pic}^{0}(X_{n})[p^{\infty}]

becomes a module over the Iwasawa algebra
\Lambda
of
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, and one can use the structure theorem for finitely generated
\Lambda
-modules up to pseudo-isomorphisms to obtain a module theoretical proof of (
1.2
).

The two approaches are intimately related to one another. Using the usual non-canonical isomorphism
\Lambda\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathbb{Z}_{p}\llbracket T\rrbracket
given by
\gamma\mapsto 1+T
, every
\Lambda
-module can be viewed as a
\mathbb{Z}_{p}\llbracket T\rrbracket
-module, and Kleine and Müller proved (
[
KM24
, Theorem 5.2 and Remark 5.3]
) that

(1.4)

{\rm char}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}\llbracket T\rrbracket}({\rm Pic}^{0}_{\Lambda})\cdot(T)=(f(T)),

where
{\rm char}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}\llbracket T\rrbracket}(M)
denotes the characteristic ideal of a finitely generated
\mathbb{Z}_{p}\llbracket T\rrbracket
-module
M
and
f(T)
is the power series constructed in
[
Val21
,
MV23
,
MV24
]
. For the module theoretical approach to both (
1.2
) and (
1.4
), in addition to
[
Gon22
]
and
[
KM24
]
, see also
[
Kat24
]
.

Remark 1.1
.

The equality (
1.4
) is not quite true, since the non-canonical isomorphism
\gamma\mapsto 1-T
was used in
[
MV24
]
instead of
\gamma\mapsto 1+T
. So it is true when replacing
T
with
-T
on the right hand side of (
1.4
).

In the analogy between graphs and compact Riemann surfaces, covering maps of graphs correspond to unramified covers of compact Riemann surfaces. On the other hand, branched (or ramified) covers of compact Riemann surfaces correspond to harmonic morphisms, a broader class of morphisms of graphs than covering maps. Harmonic morphisms were introduced in
[
Ura00
]
and studied further by many authors including for instance in
[
BN09
]
and
[
Cor12
]
. The goal of this paper is to study more general
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-towers of graphs than the ones that have been considered so far in the literature. We will consider towers

X=X_{0}\leftarrow X_{1}\leftarrow X_{2}\leftarrow\ldots\leftarrow X_{n}\leftarrow\ldots

such as (
1.1
) above, but where the maps
X_{n+1}\rightarrow X_{n}
will be
branched
(or
ramified
) covers of finite graphs rather than usual (unramified) covering maps. Branched covers of graphs form a class of graph morphisms that lies in between (unramified) covering maps and harmonic morphisms. We explain in
Section

4.3
how to construct such branched
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-towers of finite connected graphs by modifying the notion of voltage assignment accordingly. In this paper, we use the module theoretical approach. There should be a connection with Ihara zeta and
L
-functions as well in this situation, as studied for instance in
[
Bas92
]
and
[
Zak21
]
, and we would like to look at this point of view in a separate work. The reason why we adopt the module theoretical approach here is that given a branched cover of finite connected graphs
Y\rightarrow X
, it induces a natural surjective group morphism
{\rm Pic}^{0}(Y)\rightarrow{\rm Pic}^{0}(X)
just as in the unramified situation. Therefore, starting with a branched
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-tower of graphs, we obtain again a compatible system of morphisms
{\rm Pic}^{0}(X_{n+1})[p^{\infty}]\rightarrow{\rm Pic}^{0}(X_{n})[p^{\infty}]
, and we can consider the
\Lambda
-module

{\rm Pic}_{\Lambda}^{0}=\varprojlim_{n\geq 0}{\rm Pic}^{0}(X_{n})[p^{\infty}].

The study of this Iwasawa module leads us to our first main result.

Theorem A

(
Theorem

5.6
)
.

Let

X=X_{0}\leftarrow X_{1}\leftarrow X_{2}\leftarrow\ldots\leftarrow X_{n}\leftarrow\ldots

be a branched
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-tower of finite connected graphs arising from a voltage assignment as explained in
Section

4.3
. Then
{\rm Pic}_{\Lambda}^{0}
is a finitely generated torsion
\Lambda
-module. Moreover, letting
\mu=\mu({\rm Pic}_{\Lambda}^{0})
and
\lambda=\lambda({\rm Pic}_{\Lambda}^{0})
, there exist
n_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and
\nu\in\mathbb{Z}
such that

{\rm ord}_{p}(\kappa(X_{n}))=\mu p^{n}+\lambda n+\nu,

when
n\geq n_{0}
.

We then move on to study the characteristic ideal of
{\rm Pic}_{\Lambda}^{0}
, and we prove the following result which we now state in an imprecise form. See
Theorem

5.9
and
Theorem

6.1
for the precise formulation.

Theorem B

(
Theorem

5.9
and
Theorem

6.1
)
.

Let

X=X_{0}\leftarrow X_{1}\leftarrow X_{2}\leftarrow\ldots\leftarrow X_{n}\leftarrow\ldots

be a branched
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-tower of finite connected graphs arising from a voltage assignment as explained in
Section

4.3
. We define an operator
\Delta
on a free
\mathbb{Z}_{p}\llbracket T\rrbracket
-module
M
of finite rank for which

{\rm char}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}\llbracket T\rrbracket}({\rm Pic_{\Lambda}^{0}})\cdot(T)=({\rm det}(\Delta)).

Moreover,
{\rm det}(\Delta)\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}\llbracket T\rrbracket
can be explicitly calculated in terms of the voltage assignment.

The paper is organized as follows. In
Section

2
, we gather together some basic facts that will be used throughout the paper. We remind the reader about Serre’s formalism for graphs in
Section

2.1
, about the Picard group of degree zero in
Section

2.2
, and about group acting on graphs in
Section

2.3
. When a group acts on a graph, the theory becomes an equivariant one, and we explain this for the Picard group in
Section

2.4
. Ultimately, we are interested in the
p
-part of the number of spanning trees, and thus we tensor everything with
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
over
\mathbb{Z}
in
Section

2.5
. We remind the reader about the Iwasawa algebra and Iwasawa modules in
Section

2.6
. In
Section

3
, we introduce the notion of branched cover of graphs
Y\rightarrow X
, and we explain a useful induced map on the Picard groups
{\rm Pic}^{0}(Y)\rightarrow{\rm Pic}^{0}(X)
. In
Section

4
, we introduce the branched
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-towers of graphs that we will be studying in this paper. We construct branched covers explicitly using voltage assignments in
Section

4.1
and we explain how this construction behaves functorially in
Section

4.2
. This allows us to construct branched
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-towers of finite graphs in
Section

4.3
. To every branched
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-tower of finite graphs is associated an unramified
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-tower. This is explained in
Section

4.4
,
Section

4.5
, and
Section

4.6
. In
Section

5
, we study the Iwasawa module
{\rm Pic}_{\Lambda}^{0}
associated to a branched
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-tower of finite connected graphs. We prove the analogue of Iwasawa’s asymptotic class number formula in
Section

5.1
, and we find a generator for the characteristic ideal of
{\rm Pic}_{\Lambda}^{0}
in
Section

5.2
. This allows us to end this paper with a few numerical examples in
Section

6
.

2.
Preliminaries

2.1.
Graph theory

Throughout this paper, we use Serre’s formalism for graphs (see
[
Ser77
]
and
[
Sun13
]
). Thus, a graph
X
consists of a vertex set
V_{X}
and a set of directed edges
\mathbf{E}_{X}
equipped with an incidence function
{\rm inc}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbf{E}_{X}\rightarrow V_{X}\times V_{X}
given by
e\mapsto{\rm inc}(e)=(o(e),t(e))
and an inversion function
{\rm inv}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbf{E}_{X}\rightarrow\mathbf{E}_{X}
also denoted by
e\mapsto\bar{e}
satisfying the following conditions:

(1)

\bar{e}\neq e
,

(2)

\bar{\bar{e}}=e
,

(3)

o(\bar{e})=t(e)
and
t(\bar{e})=o(e)
,

for all
e\in\mathbf{E}_{X}
. The vertex
o(e)
is called the origin and the vertex
t(e)
the terminus of the directed edge
e
. Given
v\in V_{X}
, we let

\mathbf{E}_{X,v}=\{e\in\mathbf{E}_{X}\,\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\,o(e)=v\}.

We will also write
\mathbf{E}_{X,v}^{o}
instead of
\mathbf{E}_{X,v}
at times. Similarly to
\mathbf{E}_{X,v}^{o}=\mathbf{E}_{X,v}
, one can define

\mathbf{E}_{X,v}^{t}=\{e\in\mathbf{E}_{X}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}t(e)=v\},

and the inversion map induces bijections

{\rm inv}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbf{E}_{X,v}^{o}\rightarrow\mathbf{E}_{X,v}^{t}

for all
v\in V_{X}
. The set of undirected edges is obtained by identifying
e
with
\bar{e}
and will be denoted by
E_{X}
. An orientation
S
for
X
consists of the image of a section for the natural map
\mathbf{E}_{X}\rightarrow E_{X}
. In other words, an orientation is obtained by choosing a direction for each undirected edge. A graph is finite if both
V_{X}
and
\mathbf{E}_{X}
are finite sets, and locally finite if
\mathbf{E}_{X,v}
is finite for all
v\in V_{X}
. In this case, we let

{\rm val}_{X}(v)=|\mathbf{E}_{X,v}|=|\mathbf{E}_{X,v}^{t}|,

and this quantity is called the valency (or degree) of the vertex
v
. A path in
X
is a sequence of directed edges
c=e_{1}\cdot\ldots\cdot e_{n}
for which one has
t(e_{i})=o(e_{i+1})
for all
i=1,\ldots,n-1
. The vertices
o(e_{1})
and
t(e_{n})
are called the origin and the terminus of the path
c
and will be denoted by
o(c)
and
t(c)
, respectively. The graph
X
is called connected if given any two distinct vertices
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
of
X
, there exists a path
c
in
X
satisfying
o(c)=v_{1}
and
t(c)=v_{2}
.

Definition 2.1
.

Let
X
and
Y
be graphs. A morphism
f\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\rightarrow Y
of graphs consists of two functions
f_{V}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}V_{X}\rightarrow V_{Y}
and
f_{E}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbf{E}_{X}\rightarrow\mathbf{E}_{Y}
satisfying

(1)

f_{V}(o(e))=o(f_{E}(e))
,

(2)

f_{V}(t(e))=t(f_{E}(e))
,

(3)

\overline{f_{E}(e)}=f_{E}(\bar{e})
,

for all
e\in\mathbf{E}_{X}
.

We will usually denote both
f_{V}
and
f_{E}
simply by
f
. Note that given a morphism of graphs
f\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\rightarrow Y
and any vertex
v\in V_{X}
, the restriction
f|_{\mathbf{E}_{X,v}}
induces a function

f|_{\mathbf{E}_{X,v}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbf{E}_{X,v}\rightarrow\mathbf{E}_{Y,f(v)}.

Remark 2.2
.

There are more general notions of morphisms of graphs in the literature than Definition
2.1
for which one allows edges to be mapped to vertices. See for instance
[
Cor12
, Definition 2.1]
in the case where the graphs are assumed to have no loops. In this paper, we restrict ourselves to Definition
2.1
.

2.2.
The Picard group of degree zero

Let
X=(V_{X},\mathbf{E}_{X})
be a graph. We define
{\rm Div}(X)
to be the free abelian group on
V_{X}
, and we have a natural surjective group morphism
s\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}{\rm Div}(X)\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{Z}
defined on vertices via
v\mapsto s(v)=1
. We let
{\rm Div}^{0}(X)
be the kernel of
s
so that we have a short exact sequence of abelian groups

(2.1)

0\rightarrow{\rm Div}^{0}(X)\rightarrow{\rm Div}(X)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s}}{{\rightarrow}}\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow 0.

If
X
is locally finite, then we define a few operators on
{\rm Div}(X)
as follows. The valency (or degree) operator
\mathcal{D}_{X}
is defined on vertices via

v\mapsto\mathcal{D}_{X}(v)={\rm val}_{X}(v)v,

whereas the adjacency operator
\mathcal{A}_{X}
is defined on vertices via

v\mapsto\mathcal{A}_{X}(v)=\sum_{v\in\mathbf{E}_{X,v}}t(e).

The Laplacian operator
\mathcal{L}_{X}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}{\rm Div}(X)\rightarrow{\rm Div}(X)
is then defined to be

\mathcal{L}_{X}=\mathcal{D}_{X}-\mathcal{A}_{X}.

Moreover, we define
{\rm Pr}(X)={\rm Im}(\mathcal{L}_{X})
. It is simple to check from the definitions that
{\rm Pr}(X)
is a subgroup of
{\rm Div}^{0}(X)
. In what follows, we let

{\rm Pic}(X)={\rm Div}(X)/{\rm Pr}(X)\text{ and }{\rm Pic}^{0}(X)={\rm Div}^{0}(X)/{\rm Pr}(X).

We shall refer to
{\rm Pic}(X)
as the Picard group of
X
, and to
{\rm Pic}^{0}(X)
as the Picard group of degree zero of
X
.

Theorem 2.3
.

If
X
is a finite connected graph, then one has a short exact sequence of abelian groups

(2.2)

0\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}\sum_{v\in V_{X}}v\longrightarrow{\rm Div}(X)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{L}_{X}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\rm Pr}(X)\longrightarrow 0,

so that
{\rm rank}_{\mathbb{Z}}({\rm Pr}(X))={\rm rank}_{\mathbb{Z}}({\rm Div}^{0}(X))=|V_{X}|-1
from which it follows that
{\rm Pic}^{0}(X)
is a finite abelian group. Moreover
|{\rm Pic}^{0}(X)|=\kappa(X)
, where
\kappa(X)
is the number of spanning trees of
X
.

Proof.

On one hand, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{X}\left(\sum_{v\in V_{X}}v\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{v\in V_{X}}{\rm val}_{X}(v)v-\sum_{v\in V_{X}}\sum_{e\in\mathbf{E}_{X,v}}t(e)

\displaystyle=\sum_{v\in V_{X}}{\rm val}_{X}(v)v-\sum_{e\in\mathbf{E}_{X}}t(e)

\displaystyle=\sum_{v\in V_{X}}{\rm val}_{X}(v)v-\sum_{v\in V_{X}}\sum_{e\in\mathbf{E}_{X,v}^{t}}v

\displaystyle=0.

On the other hand, if
D=\sum_{v\in V_{X}}m_{v}v\in{\rm ker}(\mathcal{L}_{X})
, then a simple calculation shows that

m_{v}=\frac{1}{{\rm val}_{X}(v)}\sum_{e\in\mathbf{E}_{X,v}^{t}}m_{o(e)}

for all
v\in V_{X}
. Choosing
v
so that
m_{v}
is maximal implies that
m_{o(e)}=m_{v}
for all
e\in\mathbf{E}_{X,v}^{t}
, and the connectedness assumption allows us to deduce that

D\in\mathbb{Z}\sum_{v\in V_{X}}v.

This shows that (
2.2
) is a short exact sequence. The fact that
|{\rm Pic}^{0}(X)|=\kappa(X)
follows from a classical theorem of Kirchhoff in graph theory. See any book on graph theory such as
[
CP18
]
.
∎

2.3.
Groups acting on graphs

The group of automorphisms of a graph
Y
will be denoted as usual by
{\rm Aut}(Y)
. Throughout this paper, we will often have groups acting on graphs. The precise definition is as follows.

Definition 2.4
.

Let
G
be a group and
Y
a graph. We say that
G
acts on
Y
if we are given a group morphism
G\rightarrow{\rm Aut}(Y)
. The group
G
is said to act without inversion if for all
e\in\mathbf{E}_{Y}
and all
\sigma\in G
, one has
\sigma\cdot e\neq\bar{e}
.

If
G
acts without inversion on a graph
Y
, then one obtains a graph
G\backslash Y
by letting the vertices to be
G\backslash V_{Y}
and the directed edges to be
G\backslash\mathbf{E}_{Y}
. The incidence map is given by
o(G\cdot e)=G\cdot o(e)
,
t(G\cdot e)=G\cdot t(e)
, and the inversion map by
\overline{G\cdot e}=G\cdot\bar{e}
. We leave it to the reader to check that
G\backslash Y
is a graph, and that the natural map
Y\rightarrow G\backslash Y
given for
w\in V_{Y}
and
e\in\mathbf{E}_{Y}
by

w\mapsto G\cdot w\text{ and }e\mapsto G\cdot e

is a morphism of graphs.

2.4.
The Picard group of degree zero as a
\mathbb{Z}[G]
-module

Let
Y
be a graph and assume that a group
G
acts on
Y
without inversion. Then,
{\rm Div}(Y)
is acted upon by
G
as well, and the short exact sequence

0\rightarrow{\rm Div}^{0}(Y)\rightarrow{\rm Div}(Y)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s}}{{\rightarrow}}\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow 0

becomes one of
\mathbb{Z}[G]
-modules, where
G
acts trivially on
\mathbb{Z}
. Note that every
\sigma\in G
induces a bijection

(2.3)

\mathbf{E}_{Y,w}\rightarrow\mathbf{E}_{Y,\sigma(w)}

for all
w\in V_{Y}
, so that if
Y
is locally finite, one has

(2.4)

{\rm val}_{Y}(\sigma\cdot w)={\rm val}_{Y}(w),

for all
w\in V_{Y}
and all
\sigma\in G
.

Proposition 2.5
.

Assuming that
Y
is locally finite, the degree, the adjacency, and the Laplacian operators on
{\rm Div}(Y)
are all morphisms of
\mathbb{Z}[G]
-modules.

Proof.

The degree operator
\mathcal{D}_{Y}
is
G
-equivariant because of (
2.4
) above. The adjacency operator is
G
-equivariant, since

\sum_{e\in\mathbf{E}_{Y,\sigma\cdot w}}t(e)=\sigma\cdot\sum_{e\in\mathbf{E}_{Y,w}}t(e)

by (
2.3
) above. It then follows that the Laplacian operator
\mathcal{L}_{Y}
is
G
-equivariant as well, since
\mathcal{L}_{Y}=\mathcal{D}_{Y}-\mathcal{A}_{Y}
.
∎

As a direct consequence, we obtain the following result.

Corollary 2.6
.

Let
Y
be a locally finite graph, and let
G
be a group acting on
Y
without inversion. Then, both
{\rm Pic}(Y)
and
{\rm Pic}^{0}(Y)
are
\mathbb{Z}[G]
-modules.

2.5.
Tensoring with
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
over
\mathbb{Z}

Later on, we will be working over
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, rather than over
\mathbb{Z}
, where
p
is a fixed rational prime. Since
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
is flat over
\mathbb{Z}
, everything we did so far works equally well over
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
. If
M
is a
\mathbb{Z}
-module, then we let

M_{p}=\mathbb{Z}_{p}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}M.

For instance, if
X
is a graph, then we have
{\rm Div}_{p}(X)
,
{\rm Div}_{p}^{0}(X),{\rm Pr}_{p}(X),{\rm Pic}_{p}(X)
, and
{\rm Pic}_{p}^{0}(X)
whenever these groups are defined. After tensoring the short exact sequence (
2.1
) with
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
over
\mathbb{Z}
, one gets

(2.5)

0\longrightarrow{\rm Div}_{p}^{0}(X)\longrightarrow{\rm Div}_{p}(X)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s_{p}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathbb{Z}_{p}\longrightarrow 0.

If
X
is locally finite, then the two short exact sequences

0\rightarrow{\rm Pr}(X)\rightarrow{\rm Div}(X)\rightarrow{\rm Pic}(X)\rightarrow 0,

and

0\rightarrow{\rm Pr}(X)\rightarrow{\rm Div}^{0}(X)\rightarrow{\rm Pic}^{0}(X)\rightarrow 0

becomes after tensoring with
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
over
\mathbb{Z}
the short exact sequences

0\rightarrow{\rm Pr}_{p}(X)\rightarrow{\rm Div}_{p}(X)\rightarrow{\rm Pic}_{p}(X)\rightarrow 0,

and

0\rightarrow{\rm Pr}_{p}(X)\rightarrow{\rm Div}_{p}^{0}(X)\rightarrow{\rm Pic}_{p}^{0}(X)\rightarrow 0

of
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-modules. If furthermore
X
is finite and connected, then the short exact sequence (
2.2
) becomes

0\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{p}\sum_{v\in V_{X}}v\longrightarrow{\rm Div}_{p}(X)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{L}_{p}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\rm Pr}_{p}(X)\longrightarrow 0.

In this situation, note that we have

{\rm Pic}_{p}^{0}(X)\simeq{\rm Div}_{p}^{0}(X)/{\rm Pr}_{p}(X)\simeq{\rm Pic}^{0}(X)[p^{\infty}],

where
{\rm Pic}^{0}(X)[p^{\infty}]
denotes the Sylow
p
-subgroup of the finite abelian group
{\rm Pic}^{0}(X)
. In particular, we have

|{\rm Pic}_{p}^{0}(X)|=\kappa_{p}(X),

where
\kappa_{p}(X)=p^{{\rm ord}_{p}(\kappa(X))}
. In other words, tensoring with
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
over
\mathbb{Z}
allows us to focus on the
p
-part of the invariant
\kappa(X)
. If moreover the graph
X
is acted upon by a group
G
, then all these
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-modules become modules over the group ring
\mathbb{Z}_{p}[G]
.

2.6.
The Iwasawa algebra and Iwasawa modules

In this section, we gather some well-known results on the Iwasawa algebra
\Lambda
of
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
and finitely generated modules over
\Lambda
that we will use throughout. Our main references for this section are
[
Bou65
]
,
[
Was97
]
, and
[
NSW08
]
.

We prefer to work with a multiplicative notation, so we let
\Gamma
be a multiplicative group that is topologically isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
. We fix once and for all a topological generator
\gamma
for
\Gamma
. For each integer
n\geq 0
, we set
\Gamma_{n}=\Gamma/\Gamma^{p^{n}}
and we let also
\gamma_{n}=\gamma^{p^{n}}
. The Iwasawa algebra
\Lambda
is the profinite completion of the group ring
R=\mathbb{Z}_{p}[\Gamma]
, in other words

\Lambda=\mathbb{Z}_{p}\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket=\varprojlim_{n\geq 0}\mathbb{Z}_{p}[\Gamma_{n}],

where the compatible maps
\mathbb{Z}_{p}[\Gamma_{n+1}]\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{p}[\Gamma_{n}]
are the natural projection maps. It is a unique factorization domain that is a noetherian ring of Krull dimension two. Moreover it is a local ring with unique maximal ideal given by
\mathfrak{m}=(p,\gamma-1)
. The ring
\Lambda
is a topological ring when endowed with the
\mathfrak{m}
-adic topology which is compact, and hence also complete.

The projection maps
\phi_{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{Z}_{p}[\Gamma]\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{p}[\Gamma_{n}]
induce a unital ring morphism
\mathbb{Z}_{p}[\Gamma]\rightarrow\Lambda
which is injective, since
\bigcap_{n\geq 0}{\rm ker}(\phi_{n})=0
. Thus, we have a natural embedding of unital commutative rings
R\hookrightarrow\Lambda
. Throughout, we let

\omega_{n}=\gamma_{n}-1=\gamma^{p^{n}}-1\in\mathfrak{m}^{n}\subseteq R\subseteq\Lambda.

The natural projection maps
\Lambda\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{p}[\Gamma_{n}]
and
R\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{p}[\Gamma_{n}]
induce isomorphisms

(2.6)

\Lambda/\omega_{n}\Lambda\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathbb{Z}_{p}[\Gamma_{n}]\text{ and }R/R\cap\omega_{n}\Lambda\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathbb{Z}_{p}[\Gamma_{n}]

of unital commutative rings. In addition to the elements
\omega_{n}
, we will need the elements

\omega_{n,k}=\frac{\omega_{n}}{\omega_{k}}=1+\gamma_{k}+\gamma_{k}^{2}+\ldots+\gamma_{k}^{p^{n-k}-1}\in R,

whenever
k,n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
are such that
k\leq n
.

Any
\Lambda
-module
M
is in fact a topological
\Lambda
-module by taking for a basis of neighborhoods around
m\in M
, the sets
m+\mathfrak{m}^{n}\cdot M
, where
n\geq 0
. With this topology, any morphism of
\Lambda
-modules is automatically continuous. A finitely generated module over the Iwasawa algebra
\Lambda
is called an Iwasawa module, and any such module is compact. We can view
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
as a
\Lambda
-module or an
R
-module with trivial action. As such, we have in particular

(2.7)

\mathbb{Z}_{p}\simeq\Lambda/\omega_{0}\Lambda\text{ and }\mathbb{Z}_{p}\simeq R/R\cap\omega_{0}\Lambda.

A morphism of Iwasawa modules
f\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}M\rightarrow N
is called a pseudo-isomorphism if it has finite kernel and cokernel. This defines an equivalence relation on the collection of torsion Iwasawa modules, and we will write
M\sim N
if
M
and
N
are pseudo-isomorphic torsion Iwasawa modules. To every torsion Iwasawa module
M
is associated its Iwasawa invariants
\mu(M)
and
\lambda(M)
which are non-negative integers. Two pseudo-isomorphic torsion Iwasawa modules have the same Iwasawa invariants.

Theorem 2.7
.

Let
M
be an Iwasawa module and assume that there exists
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
such that
M/\omega_{n,k}M
is finite for all
n\geq k
. Then
M
is a torsion Iwasawa module. Moreover, there exist
n_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and
\nu\in\mathbb{Z}
such that

{\rm ord}_{p}(|M/\omega_{n,k}M|)=\mu(M)p^{n}+\lambda(M)n+\nu,

when
n\geq n_{0}
.

Proof.

See
[
Was97
, Theorem 13.19]
and
[
Was97
, Lemma 13.21]
.
∎

If
M
is a torsion Iwasawa module, then we shall denote its characteristic ideal by
{\rm char}_{\Lambda}(M)
. The characteristic ideal is also an invariant that depends only on the pseudo-isomorphism class of the torsion Iwasawa module. If

0\rightarrow M_{1}\rightarrow M_{2}\rightarrow M_{3}\rightarrow 0

is a short exact sequence of torsion Iwasawa modules, then one has

(2.8)

{\rm char}_{\Lambda}(M_{2})={\rm char}_{\Lambda}(M_{1})\cdot{\rm char}_{\Lambda}(M_{3}).

The characteristic ideal can sometimes be calculated as follows.

Theorem 2.8
.

Let
F
be a free
\Lambda
-module of finite rank and let
M
be a finitely generated torsion Iwasawa module. If we have a short exact sequence

0\rightarrow F\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f}}{{\longrightarrow}}F\longrightarrow M\longrightarrow 0

of
\Lambda
-modules, then

{\rm char}_{\Lambda}(M)=({\rm det}(f)).

Proof.

See
[
Bou65
, Chapitre VII, §4, Corollaire to Proposition 14]
.
∎

There is a non-canonical isomorphism

(2.9)

\Lambda\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathbb{Z}_{p}\llbracket T\rrbracket

given by
\gamma\mapsto 1+T
. Therefore, any
\Lambda
-module can be viewed as a
\mathbb{Z}_{p}\llbracket T\rrbracket
-module, and Iwasawa modules are in particular finitely generated
\mathbb{Z}_{p}\llbracket T\rrbracket
-modules. Every Iwasawa module
M
is pseudo-isomorphic to an Iwasawa module of the form

(2.10)

\mathbb{Z}_{p}\llbracket T\rrbracket^{r}\oplus\bigoplus_{i=1}^{s}\mathbb{Z}_{p}\llbracket T\rrbracket/(p^{m_{i}})\oplus\bigoplus_{j=1}^{t}\mathbb{Z}_{p}\llbracket T\rrbracket/(f_{j}(T)^{n_{j}}),

where
r,s,t,m_{i},n_{j}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and the
f_{j}(T)\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}[T]
are distinguished and irreducible polynomials. Recall that a polynomial
f(T)\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}[T]
is called distinguished if it is monic and
p
divides every coefficient except the leading one. For instance, the elements

\omega_{n}(T)=(1+T)^{p^{n}}-1\in\mathbb{Z}[T]\subseteq\mathbb{Z}_{p}\llbracket T\rrbracket

obtained from the
\omega_{n}
via the isomorphism (
2.9
) are distinguished. An Iwasawa module is torsion precisely when
r=0
in (
2.10
) above. If
M
is a torsion Iwasawa module pseudo-isomorphic to an Iwasawa module of the form

\bigoplus_{i=1}^{s}\mathbb{Z}_{p}\llbracket T\rrbracket/(p^{m_{i}})\oplus\bigoplus_{j=1}^{t}\mathbb{Z}_{p}\llbracket T\rrbracket/(f_{j}(T)^{n_{j}}),

then its characteristic ideal, as an ideal of
\mathbb{Z}_{p}\llbracket T\rrbracket
, is given by

{\rm char}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}\llbracket T\rrbracket}(M)=(f(T)),

where

(2.11)

f(T)=\prod_{i=1}^{s}p^{m_{i}}\cdot\prod_{j=1}^{t}f_{j}(T)^{n_{j}}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}[T].

The Iwasawa invariants of
M
are given by

\mu(M)=\mu(f(T))\text{ and }\lambda(M)=\lambda(f(T)),

where we recall that given any

g(T)=a_{0}+a_{1}T+a_{2}T^{2}+\ldots\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}\llbracket T\rrbracket,

one sets

\mu(g(T))={\rm min}\{{\rm ord}_{p}(a_{i})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}i\geq 0\},

and

\lambda(g(T))={\rm min}\{i\geq 0\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}{\rm ord}_{p}(a_{i})=\mu(g(T))\}.

For the polynomial
f(T)
of (
2.11
) above, one has

\mu(f(T))=\sum_{i=1}^{s}m_{i},

and

\lambda(f(T))={\rm deg}(f(T)),

since the
f_{j}(T)
are distinguished.

3.
Branched covers of graphs

3.1.
Branched covers

The following definition is taken from
[
Sun13
, page 69]
.

Definition 3.1
.

Let
X
and
Y
be graphs and let
p\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Y\rightarrow X
be a morphism of graphs. Then
p
is called a branched (or ramified) cover if the following two conditions are satisfied:

(1)

Both functions
p\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}V_{Y}\rightarrow V_{X}
and
p\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbf{E}_{Y}\rightarrow\mathbf{E}_{X}
are surjective,

(2)

For all
w\in V_{Y}
, the cardinality
m_{w}
, also denoted by
m_{p}(w)
, of
(p|_{\mathbf{E}_{Y,w}})^{-1}(e)
is independent of
e\in\mathbf{E}_{X,p(w)}
. The cardinality
m_{w}
is called the ramification index of the vertex
w
. If
m_{w}
is a positive integer, then the function

p|_{\mathbf{E}_{Y,w}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbf{E}_{Y,w}\rightarrow\mathbf{E}_{X,p(w)}

is
m_{w}
-to-
1
.

If
p\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Y\rightarrow X
is a branched cover for which
m_{w}=1
for all
w\in V_{Y}
, then we will refer to
p
as an unramified cover or simply a cover. In this case, the functions
p|_{\mathbf{E}_{Y,w}}
are bijections for all
w\in V_{Y}
. In the situation where both
X
and
Y
are locally finite and
m_{w}
is finite, we have

(3.1)

{\rm val}_{Y}(w)=m_{w}\cdot{\rm val}_{X}(p(w)).

If
X,Y,Z
are graphs and
p\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Z\rightarrow Y
,
q\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Y\rightarrow X
are both branched covers of graphs, then so is the composition
q\circ p\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Z\rightarrow X
. If
w
is a vertex of
Z
, then one has

m_{q\circ p}(w)=m_{p}(w)\cdot m_{q}(p(w)),

when all these quantities are finite. As pointed out in
[
Sun13
, page 69]
, a branched cover in the sense of Definition
3.1
is an example of a harmonic morphism as defined for instance in
[
Ura00
]
and
[
BN09
]
under suitable assumptions on the graphs involved such as simplicity or looplessness.

Remark 3.2
.

If
p\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Y\rightarrow X
is a branched cover and
Y
is connected, then so is
X
. Indeed, if
v_{1},v_{2}\in V_{X}
, then let
w_{1},w_{2}\in V_{Y}
be such that
p(w_{i})=v_{i}
for
i=1,2
. Since
Y
is connected, there exists a path
c
in
Y
going from
w_{1}
to
w_{2}
. Then, the path
p(c)
is a path in
X
going from
v_{1}
to
v_{2}
showing the claim.

Following
[
Sun13
, page 69]
, given a branched cover
p\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Y\rightarrow X
and
v\in V_{X}
, let

d(v)=\sum_{w\in p^{-1}(v)}m_{w},

provided this sum is finite. Since we have commutative diagrams

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{E}_{Y,w}^{o}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{E}_{X,p(w)}^{o}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{E}_{Y,w}^{t}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{E}_{X,p(w)}^{t},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p|_{\mathbf{E}_{Y,w}^{o}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm inv}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm inv}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p|_{\mathbf{E}_{Y,w}^{t}}}

it follows that the maps

p|_{\mathbf{E}_{Y,w}^{t}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbf{E}_{Y,w}^{t}\rightarrow\mathbf{E}_{X,p(w)}^{t}

are also
m_{w}
-to-
1
. Therefore, if
e\in\mathbf{E}_{X}
, then

d(o(e))=|p^{-1}(e)|=d(t(e)),

and if
X
is connected,
d(v)
is independent of the vertex
v\in V_{X}
. This nonnegative integer is called the degree of the branched cover
p
and is denoted by
[Y\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X]
. One has

(3.2)

[Y\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X]=\sum_{w\in p^{-1}(v)}m_{w},

for all
v\in V_{X}
, when
X
is assumed to be connected.

Whenever a group
G
acts on a graph
Y
, one gets two
G
-sets, as both
V_{Y}
and
\mathbf{E}_{Y}
are acted upon by
G
as well. Note that if
G
acts on a graph
Y
in a way that
G
acts freely on
V_{Y}
, then it necessarily acts freely on
\mathbf{E}_{Y}
as well. Indeed, if
\sigma\cdot e=e
for some
e\in\mathbf{E}_{Y}
, then
o(e)=o(\sigma\cdot e)=\sigma\cdot o(e)
from which we deduce that
\sigma
is the neutral element of
G
provided
G
acts freely on
V_{Y}
.

Proposition 3.3
.

Let
Y
be a graph and let
G
be a group acting without inversion on
Y
.

(1)

If
G
acts freely on
\mathbf{E}_{Y}
, then the natural morphism of graphs
p\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Y\rightarrow G\backslash Y
is a branched cover. Moreover, for all
w\in V_{Y}
, one has
m_{w}=|S_{w}|
, where
S_{w}={\rm Stab}_{G}(w)
.

(2)

If
G
acts freely on
V_{Y}
, then the natural morphism of graphs
p\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Y\rightarrow G\backslash Y
is an unramified cover.

Proof.

It is clear that
p
is surjective both on vertices and directed edges. For simplicity let
X=G\backslash Y
and let
G\cdot e\in\mathbf{E}_{X,p(w)}
, where
w\in V_{Y}
is an arbitrary vertex of
Y
. Then
o(G\cdot e)=G\cdot w
, and there exists
\sigma\in G
such that
o(\sigma\cdot e)=w
. A simple calculation shows that

\left(p|_{\mathbf{E}_{Y,w}}\right)^{-1}(G\cdot e)=\{\tau\cdot\sigma\cdot e\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\tau\in S_{w}\}.

Since
G
acts freely on
\mathbf{E}_{Y}
, all elements
\tau\cdot\sigma\cdot e
are distinct as
\tau
runs over
S_{w}
. This ends the proofs of both claims.
∎

3.2.
The Picard group of degree zero and branched covers

Suppose now that we have a branched cover of graphs
f\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Y\rightarrow X
, then we obtain a natural surjective group morphism

f_{*}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}{\rm Div}(Y)\rightarrow{\rm Div}(X)

given by
w\mapsto f_{*}(w)=f(w)
. If moreover
m_{w}
is finite for all
w\in V_{Y}
, then we have another natural group morphism

f_{r}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}{\rm Div}(Y)\rightarrow{\rm Div}(X)

given by
w\mapsto f_{r}(w)=m_{w}\cdot f(w)
.

Proposition 3.4
.

Assuming that
X
and
Y
are locally finite, and with the notation as above, we have a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Div}(Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Div}(Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Div}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Div}(X).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}_{X}}

Proof.

The commutativity of the diagram follows from the commutativity of the two diagrams

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Div}(Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Div}(Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Div}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Div}(X),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}_{X}}
and
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Div}(Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Div}(Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Div}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Div}(X).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}_{X}}

The first diagram commutes because of (
3.1
), and the second diagram commutes since
f|_{\mathbf{E}_{Y,w}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbf{E}_{Y,w}\rightarrow\mathbf{E}_{X,f(w)}
is
m_{w}
-to-
1
.
∎

Since
f_{*}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}{\rm Div}(Y)\rightarrow{\rm Div}(X)
is surjective, it follows from Proposition
3.4
that we have two surjective group morphisms

(3.3)

f_{*}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}{\rm Pic}(Y)\rightarrow{\rm Pic}(X)\text{ and }f_{*}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}{\rm Pic}^{0}(Y)\rightarrow{\rm Pic}^{0}(X).

The surjectivity of the second group morphism implies the divisibility
\kappa(X)\,|\,\kappa(Y)
whenever
f\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Y\rightarrow X
is a branched cover of finite connected graphs.

Remark 3.5
.

The fact that
\kappa(X)\,|\,\kappa(Y)
is known to hold true more generally for harmonic morphisms (see
[
BN09
, Section 4]
).

4.
Constructions of branched covers via voltage assignments

4.1.
The basic construction

Let
X
be a graph,
G
a group (for which we use the multiplicative notation), and
\alpha\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbf{E}_{X}\rightarrow G
a function satisfying

(4.1)

\alpha(\bar{e})=\alpha(e)^{-1}.

Such a function
\alpha\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbf{E}_{X}\rightarrow G
satisfying (
4.1
) above is often called a voltage assignment on
X
with values in the group
G
. Note that if
S
is an orientation for
X
, then it suffices to specify
\alpha
on
S
and set
\alpha(\bar{s})=\alpha(s)^{-1}
in order to get a function
\alpha\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbf{E}_{X}\rightarrow G
satisfying (
4.1
) above. Let also

\mathcal{I}=\{(v,I_{v})\,|\,v\in V_{X}\text{ and }I_{v}\leq G\},

be a collection of subgroups of
G
indexed by the vertices of
X
. We define a graph
X(G,\mathcal{I},\alpha)
as follows. The vertex set is the disjoint union

V=\bigsqcup_{v\in V_{X}}\{v\}\times G/I_{v},

and the collection of directed edges is given by

\mathbf{E}=\mathbf{E}_{X}\times G.

The directed edge
(e,\sigma)
connects the vertex
(o(e),\sigma I_{o(e)})
to the vertex
(t(e),\sigma\alpha(e)I_{t(e)})
. Furthermore, one lets

\overline{(e,\sigma)}=(\bar{e},\sigma\alpha(e)).

We leave it to the reader to check that
X(G,\mathcal{I},\alpha)
is a graph.

The group
G
acts naturally on the graph
X(G,\mathcal{I},\alpha)
without inversion. Indeed, for simplicity, let
Y=X(G,\mathcal{I},\alpha).
If
\tau\in G
, let
\phi_{\tau}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Y\rightarrow Y
be defined via

\phi_{\tau}(v,\sigma I_{v})=(v,\tau\sigma I_{v})\text{ and }\phi_{\tau}(e,\sigma)=(e,\tau\sigma).

We leave it to the reader to check that
\phi_{\tau}
is an automorphism of graphs. It is then simple to check that the map
G\rightarrow{\rm Aut}(Y)
defined via
\tau\mapsto\phi_{\tau}
is a homomorphism of groups which gives an action of
G
on
Y
without inversion. It clearly acts freely on
\mathbf{E}_{Y}
, but not necessarily freely on
V_{Y}
. In fact, if
w=(v,\sigma I_{v})\in V_{Y}
, then

S_{w}={\rm Stab}_{G}(w)=\sigma I_{v}\sigma^{-1}.

We define the map

p\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X(G,\mathcal{I},\alpha)\rightarrow X

via
p(v,\sigma\cdot I_{v})=v
and
p(e,\sigma)=e
. It is simple to verify that
p
is a morphism of graphs. In fact, it is a branched cover, since for
w=(v,\sigma I_{v})\in V_{Y}
, given
e\in\mathbf{E}_{X,v}
, one has

\left(p|_{\mathbf{E}_{Y,w}}\right)^{-1}(e)=\{(e,\rho\cdot\sigma)\,\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\,\rho\in S_{w}=\sigma I_{v}\sigma^{-1}\}.

Thus the cardinality of
\left(p|_{\mathbf{E}_{Y,w}}\right)^{-1}(e)
is
|I_{v}|
and is independent of
e\in\mathbf{E}_{X,v}
. Summarizing the previous discussion, we have the following proposition.

Proposition 4.1
.

Let
X
be a graph and
\alpha\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbf{E}_{X}\rightarrow G
a function satisfying (
4.1
). Let also
\mathcal{I}=\{(v,I_{v})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}I_{v}\leq G\}
be a collection of subgroups of
G
indexed by
V_{X}
and consider the graph
Y=X(G,\mathcal{I},\alpha)
. Then, the natural morphism of graphs

p\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Y\rightarrow X

is a branched cover of graphs. The group
G
acts naturally on
Y
without inversion and freely on
\mathbf{E}_{Y}
. Moreover, for each vertex
w\in V_{Y}
, the ramification index
m_{p}(w)
depends only on
v=p(w)\in V_{X}
and is equal to
|I_{v}|
. If
G
is finite, then (
3.2
) becomes

[Y\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X]=r_{v}\cdot m_{v},

where
[Y\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X]=|G|
,
m_{v}=|I_{v}|
and
r_{v}=|p^{-1}(v)|
for all
v\in V_{X}
.

4.2.
Functoriality

More generally, assume that
f\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}G_{1}\rightarrow G_{2}
is a surjective group morphism and that
\alpha\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbf{E}_{X}\rightarrow G_{1}
is a voltage assignment with values in
G_{1}
. Composing with
f
gives a voltage assignment
f\circ\alpha\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbf{E}_{X}\rightarrow G_{2}
with values in
G_{2}
. We let

f(\mathcal{I})=\{(v,f(I_{v}))\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}v\in V_{X}\},

so that
f(\mathcal{I})
is a collection of subgroups of
G_{2}
indexed by the vertices of
X
. We obtain two graphs

X(G_{1},\mathcal{I},\alpha)\text{ and }X(G_{2},f(\mathcal{I}),f\circ\alpha),

and there is a natural map
f_{*}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X(G_{1},\mathcal{I},\alpha)\rightarrow X(G_{2},f(\mathcal{I}),f\circ\alpha)
given by

f_{*}(v,\sigma_{1}I_{v})=(v,f(\sigma_{1})f(I_{v}))\text{ and }f_{*}(e,\sigma_{1})=(e,f(\sigma_{1})).

We leave it to the reader to check that
f_{*}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X(G_{1},\mathcal{I},\alpha)\rightarrow X(G_{2},f(\mathcal{I}),f\circ\alpha)
is a morphism of graphs that is surjective on vertices and directed edges. If fact it is a branched cover of graphs. Indeed, for simplicity let as above
Z=X(G_{1},\mathcal{I},\alpha)
, but also
Y=X(G_{2},f(\mathcal{I}),f\circ\alpha)
. Moreover, let
w=(v,\sigma_{1}I_{v})
be a vertex of
Z
. If
(e,\sigma_{2})\in\mathbf{E}_{Y,f_{*}(w)}
, then
\sigma_{2}=f(\sigma_{1}\tau)
for some
\tau\in I_{v}
. A simple calculation shows that

(4.2)

\left(f_{*}|_{\mathbf{E}_{Z,w}}\right)^{-1}((e,\sigma_{2}))=\{(e,\rho\sigma_{1}\tau)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\rho\in\sigma_{1}I_{v}\sigma_{1}^{-1}\cap{\rm ker}(f)\},

so that the second condition of
Definition

3.1
is satisfied with

m_{w}=|\sigma_{1}I_{v}\sigma_{1}^{-1}\cap{\rm ker}(f)|.

Note that if
f\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}G_{1}\rightarrow 1
is the trivial group morphism, then we get back the situation of
Proposition

4.1
.

4.3.
Branched
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-towers of graphs

We can now explain the
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-towers of graphs that we will be studying in this paper. As in
Section

2.6
, we let
\Gamma
be a multiplicative topological group isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
. Let
X=(V_{X},\mathbf{E}_{X})
be a finite connected graph and let
\alpha\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbf{E}_{X}\rightarrow\Gamma
be a function satisfying (
4.1
). Moreover, for each
v\in V_{X}
, choose a closed subgroup
I_{v}
of
\Gamma
, and as in
Section

4.1
, let
\mathcal{I}=\{(v,I_{v})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}v\in V_{X}\}
. We obtain a graph
X_{\infty}=X(\Gamma,\mathcal{I},\alpha)
and a branched cover
X_{\infty}\rightarrow X
. The graph
X_{\infty}
is infinite. In order to get finite graphs, consider for each integer
n\geq 1
the natural surjective group morphism
\pi_{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\Gamma\twoheadrightarrow\Gamma_{n}
, where recall from
Section

2.6
that we set
\Gamma_{n}=\Gamma/\Gamma^{p^{n}}\simeq\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}\mathbb{Z}
. Let
\alpha_{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbf{E}_{X}\rightarrow\Gamma_{n}
be the composition
\pi_{n}\circ\alpha
. We let also

\mathcal{I}_{n}=\{(v,\pi_{n}(I_{v}))\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}v\in V_{X}\}

which is a collection of subgroups of
\Gamma_{n}
indexed by the vertices of
X
. We obtain a family of finite graphs

X_{n}=X(\Gamma_{n},\mathcal{I}_{n},\alpha_{n}),

and the natural surjective group morphisms
\Gamma_{n+1}\twoheadrightarrow\Gamma_{n}
induces by
Section

4.2
branched covers
X_{n+1}\rightarrow X_{n}
for each
n\geq 0
. We thus obtain a tower of graphs

(4.3)

X=X_{0}\leftarrow X_{1}\leftarrow X_{2}\leftarrow\ldots\leftarrow X_{n}\leftarrow\ldots,

where each map
X_{n+1}\rightarrow X_{n}
is a branched cover satisfying
[X_{n+1}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X_{n}]=p
. We call such a tower a branched (or ramified)
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-towers of finite graphs provided all
X_{n}
are connected. We explain a sufficient condition that guarantees the connectedness of all finite graphs
X_{n}
in
Section

4.5
below. Each graph
X_{n}
in the tower (
4.3
) is acted upon by
\Gamma_{n}
by
Corollary

2.6
and
Proposition

4.1
.

Proposition 4.2
.

For the purpose of this proposition, let
f
denote the natural group morphism
{\rm Pic}^{0}(X_{n+1})\twoheadrightarrow{\rm Pic}^{0}(X_{n})
from (
3.3
) and let
p
denote the natural projection map
\Gamma_{n+1}\twoheadrightarrow\Gamma_{n}
. Then, for all
x\in{\rm Pic}^{0}(X_{n+1})
and for all
\gamma\in\Gamma_{n+1}
, one has

p(\gamma)\cdot f(x)=f(\gamma\cdot x).

The same property holds true for the group morphism
{\rm Pic}(X_{n+1})\twoheadrightarrow{\rm Pic}(X_{n})
.

Proof.

Let
w=(v,\sigma\pi_{n+1}(I_{v}))
be a vertex of
X_{n+1}
. On one hand, we have

\displaystyle f(\gamma\cdot(v,\sigma\pi_{n+1}(I_{v})))

\displaystyle=f(v,\gamma\sigma\pi_{n+1}(I_{v}))

\displaystyle=(v,p(\gamma)p(\sigma)\pi_{n}(I_{v})),

and on the other hand

\displaystyle p(\gamma)\cdot f(v,\sigma\pi_{n+1}(I_{v}))

\displaystyle=p(\gamma)(v,p(\sigma)\pi_{n}(I_{v}))

\displaystyle=(v,p(\gamma)p(\sigma)\pi_{n}(I_{v}))

showing the desired claim.
∎

The group morphisms
{\rm Pic}^{0}(X_{n+1})\rightarrow{\rm Pic}^{0}(X_{n})
induce maps
{\rm Pic}^{0}_{p}(X_{n+1})\rightarrow{\rm Pic}^{0}_{p}(X_{n})
that form a compatible system of
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-module morphisms. By
Proposition

4.2
, the projective limit

(4.4)

{\rm Pic}^{0}_{\Lambda}=\varprojlim_{n\geq 0}{\rm Pic}^{0}_{p}(X_{n}),

is a
\Lambda
-module. Similarly, the group morphisms
{\rm Pic}(X_{n+1})\rightarrow{\rm Pic}(X_{n})
induce maps after tensoring with
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
over
\mathbb{Z}
that also form a compatible system of
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-module morphisms
{\rm Pic}_{p}(X_{n+1})\rightarrow{\rm Pic}_{p}(X_{n})
. Thus, we obtain another
\Lambda
-module

(4.5)

{\rm Pic}_{\Lambda}=\varprojlim_{n\geq 0}{\rm Pic}_{p}(X_{n}).

4.4.
Immersions

In this paper, we will encounter another type of morphisms for which we now give the precise definition.

Definition 4.3
.

Let
X
and
Y
be graphs. A morphism of graphs
\iota\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\rightarrow Y
is called an immersion if the induced function

\iota|_{\mathbf{E}_{X,v}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbf{E}_{X,v}\rightarrow\mathbf{E}_{Y,\iota(v)}

is injective for all
v\in V_{X}
.

Starting with a graph
X
, a voltage assignment
\alpha\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbf{E}_{X}\rightarrow G
, and a collection of subgroups
\mathcal{I}
indexed by the vertices of
X
as in
Section

4.1
, one can forget about
\mathcal{I}
(or take
I_{v}=1
for all vertices
v\in V_{X}
), and keep the same voltage assignment
\alpha
. In this case, the morphism of graphs
p\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X(G,\alpha)\rightarrow X
is an unramified cover of graphs. Furthermore, we have a commutative diagram of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(G,\alpha)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(G,\mathcal{I},\alpha)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}

where
\iota
is the morphism of graphs given by

(v,\sigma)\mapsto\iota(v,\sigma)=(v,\sigma I_{v})\text{ and }(e,\sigma)\mapsto\iota(e,\sigma)=(e,\sigma).

Since
\iota
is a bijection on directed edges, the map
\iota
is an immersion of graphs. Note that the vertical map on the left is an unramified cover of graphs, whereas the map
p
is a branched cover as we explained above. The morphism of graphs
\iota
is clearly
G
-equivariant.

4.5.
Connectedness

We keep the same notation as in
Section

4.4
.

Lemma 4.4
.

If
X(G,\alpha)
is connected, then so is
X(G,\mathcal{I},\alpha)
.

Proof.

Let
(v_{1},\sigma_{1}I_{v_{1}})
and
(v_{2},\sigma_{2}I_{v_{2}})
be two vertices of
X(G,\mathcal{I},\alpha)
. Since
X(G,\alpha)
is assumed to be connected, there exists a path
c
in
X(G,\alpha)
going from
(v_{1},\sigma_{1})
to
(v_{2},\sigma_{2})
. Then, the path
\iota(c)
of
X(G,\mathcal{I},\alpha)
goes from
(v_{1},\sigma_{1}I_{v_{1}})
to
(v_{2},\sigma_{2}I_{v_{2}})
showing the claim.
∎

Let now
\alpha\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbf{E}_{X}\rightarrow\Gamma
be a voltage assignment and let
\mathcal{I}
be a collection of closed subgroups of
\Gamma
indexed by
V_{X}
as in
Section

4.3
. If we let
X_{n}^{unr}=X(\Gamma_{n},\alpha_{n})
, then we have an unramified
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-tower of finite graphs

X=X_{0}\leftarrow X_{1}^{unr}\leftarrow X_{2}^{unr}\leftarrow\ldots\leftarrow X_{n}^{unr}\leftarrow\ldots

that fits into a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{1}^{unr}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{2}^{unr}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{n}^{unr}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{n}}

where the vertical maps are all immersions of graphs. Letting
\pi_{1}(X,v_{0})
be the fundamental group of
X
based at a vertex
v_{0}
, the function
\alpha\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbf{E}_{X}\rightarrow\Gamma
induces a group morphism

\rho_{\alpha}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\pi_{1}(X,v_{0})\rightarrow\Gamma.

Theorem 4.5
.

With the notation as above, all the graphs
X_{n}^{unr}
are connected if and only if
\rho_{\alpha}(\pi_{1}(X,v_{0}))
generates
\Gamma
topologically.

Proof.

This follows from
[
RV23
, Theorem 2.11]
.
∎

Combined with Lemma
4.4
and the discussion in Section
2.3.1
of
[
RV23
]
, one has a sufficient condition that can be checked explicitly to construct branched
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-towers of graphs, and guarantee the connectedness of all the graphs
X_{n}
.

In
Section

5.2
below, we will also consider the infinite graph
X_{\infty}^{unr}=X(\Gamma,\alpha)
. Note that
X_{\infty}^{unr}
is locally finite, whereas
X_{\infty}
is not necessarily.

4.6.
A useful lemma

Let us keep again the same notation as in
Section

4.4
. The following lemma and corollary will be used in
Section

5
below.

Lemma 4.6
.

To simplify the notation, let
Y^{unr}=X(G,\alpha)
,
Y=X(G,\mathcal{I},\alpha)
, and assume that
X,Y
and
Y^{unr}
are all locally finite graphs. Since
\iota\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Y^{unr}\rightarrow Y
is surjective on vertices, we obtain a natural surjective group morphism

\iota_{*}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}{\rm Div}(Y^{unr})\rightarrow{\rm Div}(Y).

Assuming that
I_{v}
is finite for all
v\in V_{X}
, then for all
v\in V_{X}
and for all
\sigma\in G
, one has

\mathcal{L}_{Y}\circ\iota_{*}((v,\sigma))=\sum_{i\in I_{v}}\iota_{*}\circ\mathcal{L}_{Y^{unr}}((v,\sigma\cdot i)).

Proof.

On one hand, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{Y}\circ\iota_{*}((v,\sigma))

\displaystyle=\mathcal{L}_{Y}((v,\sigma I_{v}))

\displaystyle=\mathcal{D}_{Y}((v,\sigma I_{v}))-\mathcal{A}_{Y}((v,\sigma I_{v}))

\displaystyle=|I_{v}|{\rm val}_{X}(v)(v,\sigma I_{v})-\sum_{\varepsilon\in\mathbf{E}_{Y,(v,\sigma I_{v})}}t(\varepsilon)

\displaystyle=|I_{v}|{\rm val}_{X}(v)(v,\sigma I_{v})-\sum_{e\in\mathbf{E}_{X,v}}\sum_{\tau\in\sigma I_{v}}(t(e),\tau\alpha(e)I_{t(e)})

\displaystyle=|I_{v}|{\rm val}_{X}(v)(v,\sigma I_{v})-\sum_{e\in\mathbf{E}_{X,v}}\sum_{i\in I_{v}}(t(e),\sigma i\alpha(e)I_{t(e)}).

On the other hand, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{Y^{unr}}((v,\sigma i))

\displaystyle={\rm val}_{X}(v)(v,\sigma i)-\sum_{\varepsilon\in\mathbf{E}_{Y^{unr},(v,\sigma i)}}t(\varepsilon)

\displaystyle={\rm val}_{X}(v)(v,\sigma i)-\sum_{e\in\mathbf{E}_{X,v}}(t(e),\sigma i\alpha(e)).

Therefore,

\iota_{*}\circ\mathcal{L}_{Y^{unr}}((v,\sigma i))={\rm val}_{X}(v)(v,\sigma I_{v})-\sum_{e\in\mathbf{E}_{X,v}}(t(e),\sigma i\alpha(e)I_{t(e)})

from which it follows that

\sum_{i\in I_{v}}\iota_{*}\circ\mathcal{L}_{Y^{unr}}((v,\sigma i))=|I_{v}|{\rm val}_{X}(v)(v,\sigma I_{v})-\sum_{e\in\mathbf{E}_{X,v}}\sum_{i\in I_{v}}(t(e),\sigma i\alpha(e)I_{t(e)}),

and this ends the proof.
∎

Corollary 4.7
.

With the same notation as in
Lemma

4.6
, for each
v\in V_{X}
, let

P_{v}=\iota_{*}\circ\mathcal{L}_{Y^{unr}}((v,1_{G}))\in{\rm Div}(Y).

Then
{\rm Pr}(Y)
is generated over
\mathbb{Z}[G]
by

\sum_{i\in I_{v}}iP_{v}

as
v
runs over all vertices in
X
.

Proof.

By definition,
{\rm Pr}(Y)
is generated over
\mathbb{Z}[G]
by the divisors
\mathcal{L}_{Y}\circ\iota_{*}((v,1_{G}))
as
v
runs through
V_{X}
. The result then follows directly from
Lemma

4.6
.
∎

5.
Iwasawa theory for branched
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-towers of graphs

We keep the same notation as before. So
X=(V_{X},\mathbf{E}_{X})
is a finite connected graph,
\Gamma
is a multiplicative group that is topologically isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
and
\alpha\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbf{E}_{X}\xrightarrow{}\Gamma
is a voltage assignment. For each
v\in V_{X}
, we pick a closed subgroup
I_{v}
of
\Gamma
,
and this gives rise to a branched
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-tower of graphs

X=X_{0}\xleftarrow{}X_{1}\xleftarrow{}X_{2}\xleftarrow{}...\xleftarrow{}X_{n}\xleftarrow{}...

as explained
Section

4.3
. Recall that we always assume all of the graphs
X_{n}
to be connected. See
Section

4.5
for a sufficient criterion that guarantees this condition is satisfied.

Our goal in this section is to study the
\Lambda
-module
{\rm Pic}^{0}_{\Lambda}
that was defined above in (
4.4
). In order to do so, we will use the infinite graph
X_{\infty}=X(\Gamma,\mathcal{I},\alpha)
. The short exact sequence (
2.5
) for the graph
X_{\infty}
gives a short exact sequence

0\longrightarrow{\rm Div}^{0}_{p}(X_{\infty})\longrightarrow{\rm Div}_{p}(X_{\infty})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s_{p}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathbb{Z}_{p}\longrightarrow 0

of
\mathbb{Z}_{p}[\Gamma]
-modules, since
\Gamma
is acting on all of these modules, the action of
\Gamma
on
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
being the trivial one. Recall from
Section

2.6
that we set
R=\mathbb{Z}_{p}[\Gamma]
. Letting

{\rm Div}_{\Lambda}(X_{\infty})=\Lambda\otimes_{R}{\rm Div}_{p}(X_{\infty}),

the surjective
R
-module morphism
s_{p}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}{\rm Div}_{p}(X_{\infty})\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{p}
induces a surjective morphism

(5.1)

{\rm id}\otimes s_{p}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}{\rm Div}_{\Lambda}(X_{\infty})\rightarrow\Lambda\otimes_{R}\mathbb{Z}_{p}

of
\Lambda
-modules. We let

(5.2)

{\rm Div}_{\Lambda}^{0}(X_{\infty})={\rm ker}({\rm id}\otimes s_{p}).

It follows from (
2.7
) that

\Lambda\otimes_{R}\mathbb{Z}_{p}\simeq\Lambda\otimes_{R}(R/R\cap\omega_{0}\Lambda)\simeq\Lambda/\omega_{0}\Lambda\simeq\mathbb{Z}_{p}

as
\Lambda
-modules, where the element
\omega_{0}=\gamma_{0}-1\in R\subseteq\Lambda
was defined in
Section

2.6
. After making this identification, (
5.1
) and (
5.2
) gives us a short exact sequence

(5.3)

0\longrightarrow{\rm Div}_{\Lambda}^{0}(X_{\infty})\longrightarrow{\rm Div}_{\Lambda}(X_{\infty})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s_{\Lambda}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathbb{Z}_{p}\rightarrow 0

of
\Lambda
-modules. At the finite level, for each
n\geq 0
we also have a short exact sequence

(5.4)

0\rightarrow{\rm Div}_{p}^{0}(X_{n})\rightarrow{\rm Div}_{p}(X_{n})\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{p}\rightarrow 0

of
\mathbb{Z}_{p}[\Gamma_{n}]
-modules, since
\Gamma_{n}
acts on all of these modules, the action of
\Gamma_{n}
on
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
being the trivial one. Via the natural projection map
\Lambda\twoheadrightarrow\Lambda/\omega_{n}\Lambda\simeq\mathbb{Z}_{p}[\Gamma_{n}]
, we can actually view the short exact sequence (
5.4
) as one of
\Lambda
-modules as well. The branched cover
\pi_{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X_{\infty}\rightarrow X_{n}
induces a natural surjective morphism

\pi_{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}{\rm Div}_{p}(X_{\infty})\rightarrow{\rm Div}_{p}(X_{n})

of
R
-modules. The group
{\rm Div}_{p}(X_{\infty})
is only a module over
R
, but since
{\rm Div}_{p}(X_{n})
is a module over
\Lambda
, we obtain a natural surjective morphism

(5.5)

\pi_{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}{\rm Div}_{\Lambda}(X_{\infty})\rightarrow{\rm Div}_{p}(X_{n})

of
\Lambda
-modules which we denote by the same symbol. This map induces the commutative diagram of
\Lambda
-modules

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Div}_{\Lambda}^{0}(X_{\infty})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Div}_{\Lambda}(X_{\infty})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}_{p}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Div}_{p}^{0}(X_{n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Div}_{p}(X_{n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}_{p}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{\Lambda}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{p}}

where the right vertical map is the identity, and both horizontal sequences are exact.

The structure of
{\rm Div}_{\Lambda}(X_{\infty})
can be understood as follows. For simplicity, we write
V
instead of
V_{X}
. We let
V^{ram}
be the set of ramified vertices, that is the collection of vertices
v\in V
for which
I_{v}
is non-trivial. We also let
V^{unr}=V\smallsetminus V^{ram}
. From now on, we introduce a labeling of
V
, say
V=\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{s}\}
, where we agree that

V^{unr}=\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{r}\}\text{ and }V^{ram}=\{v_{r+1},\ldots,v_{s}\}.

For each
i=1,\ldots,s
, we let
w_{i,\infty}
be the vertex
(v_{i},I_{i})
of
V_{X_{\infty}}
, where we write
I_{i}
instead of
I_{v_{i}}
. Then, as an
R
-module we have

(5.6)

{\rm Div}_{p}(X_{\infty})=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{r}\mathbb{Z}_{p}[\Gamma]\cdot w_{i,\infty}\oplus\bigoplus_{i=r+1}^{s}\mathbb{Z}_{p}[\Gamma/I_{i}]\cdot w_{i,\infty}.

From now on, we let
k_{i}
be the non-negative integer such that
I_{i}=\Gamma^{p^{k_{i}}}
. Note that
\mathbb{Z}_{p}[\Gamma/I_{i}]\simeq\mathbb{Z}_{p}[\Gamma_{k_{i}}]
, and we have isomorphisms of
\Lambda
-modules

\Lambda\otimes_{R}\mathbb{Z}_{p}[\Gamma/I_{i}]\simeq\Lambda\otimes_{R}\mathbb{Z}_{p}[\Gamma_{k_{i}}]\simeq\Lambda\otimes_{R}(R/R\cap\omega_{k_{i}}\Lambda)\simeq\Lambda/\omega_{k_{i}}\Lambda\simeq\mathbb{Z}_{p}[\Gamma_{k_{i}}].

It follows that tensoring (
5.6
) with
\Lambda
over
R
gives

(5.7)

\displaystyle{\rm Div}_{\Lambda}(X_{\infty})

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{r}\Lambda\cdot w_{i,\infty}\oplus\bigoplus_{i=r+1}^{s}\mathbb{Z}_{p}[\Gamma_{k_{i}}]\cdot w_{i,\infty}

\displaystyle\simeq\Lambda^{r}\oplus\bigoplus_{i=r+1}^{s}\mathbb{Z}_{p}[\Gamma_{k_{i}}].

Similarly, at the finite level we set

I_{i,n}=I_{i}\cdot\Gamma^{p^{n}}/\Gamma^{p^{n}}

which is the image of
I_{i}
via the natural projection map
\pi_{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\Gamma\twoheadrightarrow\Gamma_{n}
, and we let
w_{i,n}
be the vertex
(v_{i},I_{i,n})
of
X_{n}
. We then have

{\rm Div}_{p}(X_{n})=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{r}\mathbb{Z}[\Gamma_{n}]\cdot w_{i,n}\oplus\bigoplus_{i=r+1}^{s}\mathbb{Z}_{p}[\Gamma_{n}/I_{i,n}]\cdot w_{i,n}

as
\mathbb{Z}_{p}[\Gamma_{n}]
-modules. Note that if
n\geq k_{i}
, then
I_{i,n}=\Gamma^{p^{k_{i}}}/\Gamma^{p^{n}}
.

Assumption 5.1
.

From now on, we assume that
n
is large enough so that

I_{i,n}=\Gamma^{p^{k_{i}}}/\Gamma^{p^{n}}

for all
i=r+1,\ldots,s
.

Under
Assumption

5.1
, we have

(5.8)

\displaystyle{\rm Div}_{p}(X_{n})

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{r}\mathbb{Z}[\Gamma_{n}]\cdot w_{i,n}\oplus\bigoplus_{i=r+1}^{s}\mathbb{Z}_{p}[\Gamma_{k_{i}}]\cdot w_{i,n}

\displaystyle\simeq\mathbb{Z}[\Gamma_{n}]^{r}\oplus\bigoplus_{i=r+1}^{s}\mathbb{Z}_{p}[\Gamma_{k_{i}}],

since
\Gamma_{n}/I_{i,n}\simeq\Gamma/I_{i}\simeq\Gamma_{k_{i}}
.

Note that from (
5.7
) and (
5.8
), the
\Lambda
-module morphism (
5.5
) is given by the natural projection map
\Lambda\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{p}[\Gamma_{n}]
on the unramified component and by the identity map on the ramified component.

Lemma 5.2
.

The kernel of the
\Lambda
-module morphism
\pi_{n}
from (
5.5
) is given by

{\rm ker}(\pi_{n})=\omega_{n}{\rm Div}_{\Lambda}(X_{\infty}).

Proof.

From (
5.7
) and (
5.8
), we get a short exact sequence

0\longrightarrow\bigoplus_{i=1}^{r}\omega_{n}\Lambda\cdot w_{i,\infty}\longrightarrow{\rm Div}_{\Lambda}(X_{\infty})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi_{n}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\rm Div}_{p}(X_{n})\longrightarrow 0

of
\Lambda
-modules. Because of
Assumption

5.1
, we have
\omega_{k_{i}}\,|\,\omega_{n}
. Therefore,

\omega_{n}\mathbb{Z}_{p}[\Gamma_{k_{i}}]\simeq\omega_{n}(\Lambda/\omega_{k_{i}}\Lambda)=0.

It follows that

\omega_{n}{\rm Div}_{\Lambda}(X_{\infty})=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{r}\omega_{n}\Lambda\cdot w_{i,\infty},

and the result follows.
∎

Note that it follows from (
5.6
) and (
5.7
) that the natural morphism of
R
-modules

{\rm Div}_{p}(X_{\infty})\rightarrow{\rm Div}_{\Lambda}(X_{\infty})

is injective, so that we can view divisors in
{\rm Div}_{p}(X_{\infty})
as lying in
{\rm Div}_{\Lambda}(X_{\infty})
.

Definition 5.3
.

For each
i=1,\ldots,s
, we let

(5.9)

P_{i,\infty}={\rm val}_{X}(v_{i})w_{i,\infty}-\sum_{e\in\mathbf{E}_{X,v_{i}}}(t(e),\alpha(e)I_{t(e)})\in{\rm Div}_{\Lambda}(X_{\infty}).

Moreover, we define
{\rm Pr}_{\Lambda}^{unr}
to be the
\Lambda
-submodule of
{\rm Div}_{\Lambda}(X_{\infty})
generated by

\{P_{i,\infty}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}i=1,\ldots,r\},

and we let
{\rm Pr}_{\Lambda,n}^{ram}
be the
\Lambda
-submodule of
{\rm Div}_{\Lambda}(X_{\infty})
generated by

\left\{\omega_{n,k_{i}}P_{i,\infty}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}i=r+1,\ldots,s\right\},

where the elements
\omega_{n,k_{i}}
were defined in
Section

2.6
.

Theorem 5.4
.

Under
Assumption

5.1
, the morphism
\pi_{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}{\rm Div}_{\Lambda}(X_{\infty})\rightarrow{\rm Div}_{p}(X_{n})
induces isomorphisms

{\rm Div}_{\Lambda}(X_{\infty})/N_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\rm Pic}_{p}(X_{n})\text{ and }{\rm Div}_{\Lambda}^{0}(X_{\infty})/N_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\rm Pic}_{p}^{0}(X_{n}),

of
\Lambda
-modules, where

N_{n}=\omega_{n}{\rm Div}_{\Lambda}(X_{\infty})+{\rm Pr}_{\Lambda,n}^{ram}+{\rm Pr}_{\Lambda}^{unr}.

Proof.

For each
n\geq 0
, let
P_{i,n}\in{\rm Div}_{p}(X_{n})
be the divisor
P_{v_{i}}
for the graph
X_{n}
defined in
Corollary

4.7
. Observe now that for
i=1,\ldots,r
, one has

\pi_{n}(P_{i,\infty})=P_{i,n},

whereas for
i=r+1,\ldots,s
, one has

\displaystyle\pi_{n}\left(\omega_{n,k_{i}}P_{i,\infty}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{p^{n-k_{i}}-1}\gamma^{jp^{k_{i}}}\cdot P_{i,n}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma\in I_{i,n}}\sigma P_{i,n}.

Corollary

4.7
and
Lemma

5.2
imply the equality

(5.10)

\pi_{n}(N_{n})={\rm Pr}_{p}(X_{n})

which implies also the inclusion
N_{n}\subseteq\pi_{n}^{-1}({\rm Pr}_{p}(X_{n}))
. Conversely, if
D\in\pi_{n}^{-1}({\rm Pr}_{p}(X_{n}))
, then
\pi_{n}(D)\in{\rm Pr}_{p}(X_{n})
so that by (
5.10
), one has
\pi_{n}(D)=\pi_{n}(D_{0})
for some
D_{0}\in N_{n}
.
Lemma

5.2
implies that
D-D_{0}\in\omega_{n}{\rm Div}_{\Lambda}(X_{\infty})\subseteq N_{n}
and this shows the equality

N_{n}=\pi_{n}^{-1}({\rm Pr}_{p}(X_{n})).

The first isomorphism then follows.

For the second one, it suffices to notice that
\omega_{n}{\rm Div}_{\Lambda}(X_{\infty})\subseteq{\rm Div}_{\Lambda}^{0}(X_{\infty})
, and that from the definition of
P_{i,\infty}
above in (
5.9
), we also clearly have
P_{i,\infty}\in{\rm Div}_{\Lambda}^{0}(X_{\infty})
for all
i=1,\ldots,s
. Therefore,
N_{n}\subseteq{\rm Div}_{\Lambda}^{0}(X_{\infty})
for all
n\geq 0
, and this concludes the proof.
∎

As a consequence, we obtain the following concrete descriptions of
{\rm Pic}_{\Lambda}
and
{\rm Pic}_{\Lambda}^{0}
that were defined above in (
4.5
) and (
4.4
).

Corollary 5.5
.

With the same notation as above, one has

{\rm Pic}_{\Lambda}\simeq{\rm Div}_{\Lambda}(X_{\infty})/{\rm Pr}_{\Lambda}^{unr}\text{ and }{\rm Pic}_{\Lambda}^{0}\simeq{\rm Div}_{\Lambda}^{0}(X_{\infty})/{\rm Pr}_{\Lambda}^{unr}.

Proof.

As in
Theorem

5.4
, we let

N_{n}=\omega_{n}{\rm Div}_{\Lambda}(X_{\infty})+{\rm Pr}_{\Lambda,n}^{ram}+{\rm Pr}_{\Lambda}^{unr}

as long as
n\geq k={\rm max}\{k_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}i=r+1,\ldots,s\}
. Observe first that the commutativity of the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Div}_{\Lambda}(X_{\infty})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Div}_{p}(X_{n+1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Div}_{p}(X_{n})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{n+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{n}}

induces yet another commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Div}_{\Lambda}(X_{\infty})/N_{n+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Pic}_{p}(X_{n+1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Div}_{\Lambda}(X_{\infty})/N_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Pic}_{p}(X_{n}),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

where the left vertical arrow is the natural map induced from the inclusion
N_{n+1}\subseteq N_{n}
, and the two horizontal isomorphisms are given by
Theorem

5.4
. It follows that we have

{\rm Pic}_{\Lambda}\simeq\varprojlim_{n\geq k}{\rm Div}_{\Lambda}(X_{\infty})/N_{n}.

Since
{\rm Pr}_{\Lambda}^{unr}\subseteq N_{n}
, we have natural maps

{\rm Div}_{\Lambda}(X_{\infty})/{\rm Pr}_{\Lambda}^{unr}\rightarrow{\rm Div}_{\Lambda}(X_{\infty})/N_{n}

that induce a
\Lambda
-module morphism

(5.11)

{\rm Div}_{\Lambda}(X_{\infty})/{\rm Pr}_{\Lambda}^{unr}\rightarrow\varprojlim_{n\geq k}{\rm Div}_{\Lambda}(X_{\infty})/N_{n},

and it remains to show that this last morphism is in fact an isomorphism. Observe that

\bigcap_{n\geq k}N_{n}={\rm Pr}_{\Lambda}^{unr}.

Indeed, the inclusion
{\rm Pic}_{\Lambda}^{unr}\subseteq\bigcap_{n\geq k}N_{n}
is clear by definition, and if
D\in\bigcap_{n\geq k}N_{n}
, then for each
n\geq k
, we have
D=D_{n}^{\prime}+P_{n}
for some
D_{n}^{\prime}\in\omega_{n}\cdot{\rm Div}_{\Lambda}(X_{\infty})+{\rm Pr}_{\Lambda,n}^{ram}
and some
P_{n}\in{\rm Pr}_{\Lambda}^{unr}
. Since
D_{n}^{\prime}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
, we have
P_{n}\to D
as
n\to\infty
. But since
{\rm Pr}_{\Lambda}^{unr}
is finitely generated over
\Lambda
, it is compact. It follows that
D\in{\rm Pr}_{\Lambda}^{unr}
, and this shows that (
5.11
) is injective. The surjectivity of (
5.11
) follows from a standard result about projective limits of compact modules (see for instance
[
Neu99
, Chapter IV, Proposition 2.7]
). This ends the proof that

{\rm Pic}_{\Lambda}\simeq{\rm Div}_{\Lambda}(X_{\infty})/{\rm Pr}_{\Lambda}^{unr},

and the second isomorphism is proven similarly.

∎

Since
{\rm Div}_{\Lambda}(X_{\infty})
is a finitely generated
\Lambda
-module, then so is
{\rm Pic}_{\Lambda}
by
Corollary

5.5
. Moreover, since
\Lambda
is a noetherian ring,
{\rm Div}_{\Lambda}^{0}(X_{\infty})={\rm ker}(s_{\Lambda})
is a finitely generated
\Lambda
-module as well, and it follows from
Corollary

5.5
that
{\rm Pic}_{\Lambda}^{0}
is also a finitely generated
\Lambda
-module.

5.1.
The analogue of Iwasawa’s asymptotic class number formula

We can now go ahead and prove our first main theorem (
Theorem

A
from
Section

1
) of this paper.

Theorem 5.6
.

Let
X=(V_{X},\mathbf{E}_{X})
be a finite connected graph, and let
\alpha\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbf{E}_{X}\rightarrow\Gamma
be a voltage assignment. Consider a family
\mathcal{I}
of closed subgroups of
\Gamma
indexed by the vertices of
V_{X}
and consider the branched
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-tower of graphs

X=X_{0}\leftarrow X_{1}\leftarrow X_{2}\leftarrow\ldots\leftarrow X_{n}\leftarrow\ldots,

where
X_{n}=X(\Gamma_{n},\mathcal{I}_{n},\alpha_{n})
as explained in
Section

4.3
. Assume that all finite graphs
X_{n}
are connected. Then
{\rm Pic}_{\Lambda}^{0}
is a finitely generated torsion
\Lambda
-module. Moreover, if we let

\mu=\mu({\rm Pic}_{\Lambda}^{0})\text{ and }\lambda=\lambda({\rm Pic}_{\Lambda}^{0}),

then there exist
n_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and
\nu\in\mathbb{Z}
such that

{\rm ord}_{p}(\kappa(X_{n}))=\mu p^{n}+\lambda n+\nu,

when
n\geq n_{0}
.

Proof.

We keep the same notation as above. As in the proof of
Corollary

5.5
, set
k={\rm max}\{k_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}i=r+1,\ldots,s\}
, and define the
\Lambda
-modules

M={\rm Div}_{\Lambda}^{0}(X_{\infty})/{\rm Pr}_{\Lambda}^{unr}\text{ and }A=N/{\rm Pr}_{\Lambda}^{unr},

where

N=\omega_{k}{\rm Div}_{\Lambda}(X_{\infty})+\frac{\omega_{k}}{\omega_{n}}{\rm Pr}_{\Lambda,n}^{ram}+{\rm Pr}_{\Lambda}^{unr}.

Note that
M\simeq{\rm Pic}_{\Lambda}^{0}
by
Corollary

5.5
, and that
N
does not depend on
n
.
Theorem

5.4
implies that

{\rm Pic}_{p}^{0}(X_{n})\simeq M/\omega_{n,k}A,

where the elements
\omega_{n,k}\in R
were defined in
Section

2.6
. It follows that we have a short exact sequence

(5.12)

0\rightarrow A/\omega_{n,k}A\rightarrow{\rm Pic}_{p}^{0}(X_{n})\rightarrow M/A\rightarrow 0

of
\Lambda
-modules. Since
{\rm Pic}_{p}^{0}(X_{n})\simeq{\rm Pic}^{0}(X_{n})[p^{\infty}]
is finite, then so are
A/\omega_{n,k}A
and
M/A
. By definition,
A
is a finitely generated
\Lambda
-module. It follows from
Theorem

2.7
that
A
is torsion and that there exist
n_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and
\nu^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}
such that when
n\geq n_{0}
, one has

{\rm ord}_{p}(|A/\omega_{n,k}A|)=\mu(A)p^{n}+\lambda(A)n+\nu^{\prime}.

Since
M/A\simeq{\rm Div}_{\Lambda}^{0}(X_{\infty})/N
does not depend on
n
, we have
|M/A|=p^{k^{\prime}}
for some
k^{\prime}\geq 0
. Letting
\nu=\nu^{\prime}+k^{\prime}
, the short exact sequence (
5.12
) gives

{\rm ord}_{p}(\kappa(X_{n}))=\mu(A)p^{n}+\lambda(A)n+\nu,

when
n
is large. The
\Lambda
-modules
M
and
A
are related to one another via the short exact sequence

0\rightarrow A\rightarrow M\rightarrow M/A\rightarrow 0,

and since
M/A
is finite,
A
and
M
are pseudo-isomorphic. It follows that
M\simeq{\rm Pic}_{\Lambda}^{0}
is also a finitely generated torsion
\Lambda
-module and

\mu(A)=\mu(M)\text{ and }\lambda(A)=\lambda(M).

∎

Remark 5.7
.

In the situation where
V^{ram}=\varnothing
, note that we can take
k=0
and the short exact sequence (
5.12
) becomes

0\rightarrow A/\omega_{n,0}A\rightarrow{\rm Pic}_{p}^{0}(X_{n})\rightarrow{\rm Pic}_{p}^{0}(X)\rightarrow 0

for all
n\geq 0
. This corresponds to the situation originally studied from the module theoretical point of view in
[
Gon22
]
,
[
KM24
]
, and
[
Kat24
]
.

5.2.
The analogue of Iwasawa’s main conjecture

Let us start with the following proposition.

Proposition 5.8
.

Let
X=(V_{X},\mathbf{E}_{X})
be a finite connected graph, and let
\alpha\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbf{E}_{X}\rightarrow\Gamma
be a voltage assignment. Consider a family
\mathcal{I}
of closed subgroups of
\Gamma
indexed by the vertices of
V_{X}
and consider the branched
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-tower of graphs

X=X_{0}\leftarrow X_{1}\leftarrow X_{2}\leftarrow\ldots\leftarrow X_{n}\leftarrow\ldots,

where
X_{n}=X(\Gamma_{n},\mathcal{I}_{n},\alpha_{n})
as explained in
Section

4.3
. Assume that all finite graphs
X_{n}
are connected. Then, one has a short exact sequence of Iwasawa modules

0\rightarrow{\rm Pic}_{\Lambda}^{0}\rightarrow{\rm Pic}_{\Lambda}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{p}\rightarrow 0,

where
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
is viewed as a
\Lambda
-module with trivial action. Moreover,
{\rm Pic}_{\Lambda}
is a torsion Iwasawa module, and one has

{\rm char}_{\Lambda}({\rm Pic}_{\Lambda}^{0})\cdot\omega_{0}\Lambda={\rm char}_{\Lambda}({\rm Pic}_{\Lambda}).

Proof.

The short exact sequence (
5.3
) induces a short exact sequence

0\longrightarrow{\rm Div}_{\Lambda}^{0}/{\rm Pr}_{\Lambda}^{unr}\longrightarrow{\rm Div}_{\Lambda}/{\rm Pr}_{\Lambda}^{unr}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s_{\Lambda}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathbb{Z}_{p}\longrightarrow 0,

which combined with
Corollary

5.5
gives the desired short exact sequence. Since both
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
and
{\rm Pic}_{\Lambda}^{0}
are torsion Iwasawa modules, it follows that
{\rm Pic}_{\Lambda}
is also a torsion Iwasawa module. Moreover, since
{\rm char}_{\Lambda}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})=\omega_{0}\Lambda
, (
2.8
) implies that we have the equality of ideals

{\rm char}_{\Lambda}({\rm Pic}_{\Lambda}^{0})\cdot\omega_{0}\Lambda={\rm char}_{\Lambda}({\rm Pic}_{\Lambda}).

∎

It follows from
Proposition

5.8
that in order to understand
{\rm char}_{\Lambda}({\rm Pic}_{\Lambda}^{0})
, it suffices to understand
{\rm char}_{\Lambda}({\rm Pic}_{\Lambda})
. In order to do so, consider
X_{\infty}^{unr}=X(\Gamma,\alpha)
as we did at the end of
Section

4.5
. Let

M_{1}=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{r}\Lambda\cdot w_{i,\infty}^{unr}\text{ and }M_{2}=\bigoplus_{i=r+1}^{s}\Lambda\cdot w_{i,\infty}^{unr},

where
w_{i,\infty}^{unr}=(v_{i},1_{\Gamma})
for
i=1,\ldots,s
. Note that we have
{\rm Div}_{\Lambda}(X_{\infty}^{unr})=M_{1}\oplus M_{2}
. We define an operator

(5.13)

\Delta\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}{\rm Div}_{\Lambda}(X_{\infty}^{unr})\rightarrow{\rm Div}_{\Lambda}(X_{\infty}^{unr})

as follows: If
i=1,\ldots,r
, then

\Delta(w_{i,\infty}^{unr})=\mathcal{L}^{unr}_{\Lambda}(w_{i,\infty}^{unr}),

where
\mathcal{L}^{unr}_{\Lambda}
is the Laplacian operator on the locally finite graph
X_{\infty}^{unr}
, and if
i=r+1,\ldots,s
, then

\Delta(w_{i,\infty}^{unr})=\omega_{k_{i}}\cdot w_{i,\infty}^{unr}.

Since
{\rm Div}_{\Lambda}(X_{\infty}^{unr})
is a free
\Lambda
-module, it makes sense to talk about
{\rm det}(\Delta)\in\Lambda
. We are now ready to prove our second main theorem (
Theorem

B
from
Section

1
) of this paper. (See also
Theorem

6.1
below.)

Theorem 5.9
.

Let
X=(V_{X},\mathbf{E}_{X})
be a finite connected graph, and let
\alpha\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbf{E}_{X}\rightarrow\Gamma
be a voltage assignment. Consider a family
\mathcal{I}
of closed subgroups of
\Gamma
indexed by the vertices of
V_{X}
and consider the branched
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-tower of graphs

X=X_{0}\leftarrow X_{1}\leftarrow X_{2}\leftarrow\ldots\leftarrow X_{n}\leftarrow\ldots,

where
X_{n}=X(\Gamma_{n},\mathcal{I}_{n},\alpha_{n})
as explained in
Section

4.3
. Assume that all finite graphs
X_{n}
are connected. With the notation as above, one has

{\rm char}_{\Lambda}({\rm Pic}_{\Lambda})=({\rm det}(\Delta)),

where
\Delta
is the operator defined above in (
5.13
). Moreover, one has

{\rm char}_{\Lambda}({\rm Pic}_{\Lambda}^{0})\cdot\omega_{0}\Lambda=({\rm det}(\Delta)).

Proof.

Consider the natural surjective morphism

\iota_{*}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}{\rm Div}_{\Lambda}(X_{\infty}^{unr})\rightarrow{\rm Div}_{\Lambda}(X_{\infty})

of
\Lambda
-modules induced by the immersion
\iota\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X_{\infty}^{unr}\rightarrow X_{\infty}
. It follows from the explicit description (
5.7
) of the
\Lambda
-module structure of
{\rm Div}_{\Lambda}(X_{\infty})
that

{\rm ker}(\iota_{*})=\bigoplus_{i=r+1}^{s}\omega_{k_{i}}\Lambda\cdot w_{i,\infty}^{unr}.

Therefore, we have an exact sequence of
\Lambda
-modules

{\rm Div}_{\Lambda}(X_{\infty}^{unr})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Delta}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\rm Div}_{\Lambda}(X_{\infty}^{unr})\longrightarrow{\rm Div}_{\Lambda}(X_{\infty})/{\rm Pr}_{\Lambda}^{unr}\longrightarrow 0,

where the map on the right is
\iota_{*}
followed by the natural projection map
{\rm Div}_{\Lambda}(X_{\infty})\rightarrow{\rm Div}_{\Lambda}(X_{\infty})/{\rm Pr}_{\Lambda}^{unr}
. By
Corollary

5.5
, we have
{\rm Div}_{\Lambda}(X_{\infty})/{\rm Pr}_{\Lambda}^{unr}\simeq{\rm Pic}_{\Lambda}
. Moreover, since
{\rm Pic}_{\Lambda}
is a torsion
\Lambda
-module by
Proposition

5.8
and
{\rm Div}_{\Lambda}(X_{\infty}^{unr})
is free of finite rank as a
\Lambda
-module,
[
Kat24
, Lemma A.3]
implies that
\Delta
is injective so that we have a short exact sequence

0\longrightarrow{\rm Div}_{\Lambda}(X_{\infty}^{unr})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Delta}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\rm Div}_{\Lambda}(X_{\infty}^{unr})\longrightarrow{\rm Pic}_{\Lambda}\longrightarrow 0.

By
Theorem

2.8
, we have

{\rm char}_{\Lambda}({\rm Pic}_{\Lambda})=({\rm det}(\Delta)).

The second equality follows directly from
Proposition

5.8
.
∎

Remark 5.10
.

When
V^{ram}=\varnothing
,
Theorem

5.9
reduces to
[
KM24
, Theorem 5.2]
in the situation where
l=1
in their notation. In this case, we have
X_{n}=X_{n}^{unr}
for all
n\geq 0
, and
\Delta
is the Laplacian operator
\mathcal{L}_{\Lambda}^{unr}
on the locally finite graph
X_{\infty}=X_{\infty}^{unr}
.

6.
Examples

In order to provide numerical examples, it is convenient to work with
\mathbb{Z}_{p}\llbracket T\rrbracket
rather than
\Lambda
. So we now reexpress the result of
Theorem

5.9
using the non-canonical isomorphism (
2.9
). Moreover, we prefer to work additively, so we work directly with
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
rather than
\Gamma
. We will make use of the group morphism

(6.1)

\rho\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{Z}_{p}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{p}\llbracket T\rrbracket^{\times}

given by

a\mapsto\rho(a)=(1+T)^{a}=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\binom{a}{i}T^{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}\llbracket T\rrbracket^{\times},

where the
\binom{X}{i}
are the usual integer-valued polynomials given by
\binom{X}{0}=1
,
\binom{X}{1}=X
, and

\binom{X}{i}=\frac{X(X-1)\cdots(X-i+1)}{i!}\in\mathbb{Q}[X]

for
i\geq 2
. Note that the group morphism
\rho
corresponds to
\Gamma\hookrightarrow R^{\times}\hookrightarrow\Lambda^{\times}
. Starting with a voltage assignment
\alpha\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbf{E}_{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, we define a few matrices as follows. First, we let
D=(d_{ij})
be the diagonal matrix defined via

d_{ii}=\begin{cases}{\rm val}_{X}(v_{i}),&\text{ if }i=1,\ldots,r;\\
0,&\text{ otherwise. }\end{cases}

Then, we define another matrix

B(T)=(b_{ij}(T))\in M_{s\times s}(\mathbb{Z}_{p}\llbracket T\rrbracket)

as follows:

(1)

If
i=1,\ldots,s
, and
j=1,\ldots,r
, then let

b_{ij}(T)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}e\in\mathbf{E}_{X}\\
{\rm inc}(e)=(v_{j},v_{i})\end{subarray}}\rho(\alpha(e)),

where
\rho
is the group morphism (
6.1
) above.

(2)

If
i=j=r+1,\ldots,s
, then let

b_{ij}(T)=-\omega_{k_{i}}(T),

where
\omega_{k_{i}}(T)=(1+T)^{p^{k_{i}}}-1
is the distinguished polynomial in
\mathbb{Z}_{p}\llbracket T\rrbracket
corresponding to
\omega_{k_{i}}
via the isomorphism (
2.9
).

(3)

Set
b_{ij}(T)=0
otherwise.

A simple calculation based on the definition (
5.13
) of the operator
\Delta
shows that

{\rm det}(\Delta)={\rm det}(D-B(T)),

when
\Delta
is viewed as a morphism of
\mathbb{Z}_{p}\llbracket T\rrbracket
-modules. We let

(6.2)

f_{X,\mathcal{I},\alpha}(T)={\rm det}(D-B(T))\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}\llbracket T\rrbracket.

Theorem

5.9
can now be phrased as follows.

Theorem 6.1
.

Let
X=(V_{X},\mathbf{E}_{X})
be a finite connected graph, and let
\alpha\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbf{E}_{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{p}
be a voltage assignment. Consider a family
\mathcal{I}
of closed subgroups of
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
indexed by the vertices of
V_{X}
and consider the branched
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-tower of graphs

X=X_{0}\leftarrow X_{1}\leftarrow X_{2}\leftarrow\ldots\leftarrow X_{n}\leftarrow\ldots,

where
X_{n}=X(\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}\mathbb{Z},\mathcal{I}_{n},\alpha_{n})
as explained in
Section

4.3
. Assume that all finite graphs
X_{n}
are connected. Then, one has
